title: "Palworld Storms Back on Steam with 855K Concurrent Players as Version 1.0 Unlocks the World Tree and 20 new Pals"
---
Japanese developer PocketPair has just released version 1.0 of Palworld, the game that became an overnight sensation when it launched in early access in January 2024, breaking sales records left and right for a while. Judging by Steam data, it seems like the fever is back. While the game's full version did not go anywhere near the all-time peak of 2.1 million concurrent users registered at the early access launch (a figure that's still the third highest ever recorded on Valve's platform), Palworld nonetheless lured a lot of people back, with the weekend's peak reaching 855K concurrent users. Version 1.0 [&#8230;]

---
cluster_id: "20260713-0345"
published_at: "2026-07-13T21:00:01+08:00"
canonical:
  source: "IEEE Spectrum"
  url: "https://spectrum.ieee.org/nokia-phones-history"
sources:
  - source: "IEEE Spectrum"
    url: "https://spectrum.ieee.org/nokia-phones-history"
title: "Nokia’s 14 Years of Mobile-Phone Supremacy Ended in an Afternoon"
---
<img src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/two-young-girls-stand-in-front-of-a-glass-case-containing-2000-era-cellphones-behind-them-is-a-display-that-says-nokia.jpg?id=67118775&amp;width=1200&amp;height=800&amp;coordinates=0%2C750%2C0%2C750" /><br /><br /><p><strong>In 2005, Nokia sold</strong> its <a href="https://www.nigeriacommunicationsweek.com.ng/a-decade-on-nokias-record-breaking-phone/" target="_blank">billionth mobile phone</a>, a budget-friendly device that went to a customer in Nigeria. By then, the company, based in Espoo, Finland, was making one of every three cellphones globally.</p><div class="rm-embed embed-media"></div><p>But just nine years later, the mobile-device maker offloaded its entire handset division to Microsoft for pennies on the dollar, compared to what it had been worth at its peak. </p><p>Nokia had risen from obscurity in the 1990s to become a worldwide cultural phenomenon by the turn of the millennium, its signature devices featured in TV shows and movies, announcing their presence with instantly recognizable <a href="https://archive.org/details/ultimate-nokia-ringtone-collection_202304" target="_blank">Nokia ringtones</a>. </p><p>As Nokia was becoming comfortable in the spotlight, the smartphone era arrived. And what came next was swift and brutal. But, as revealed in Nokia internal documents recently made public and interviews with key Nokia engineers from that era, the company saw it coming. Within 24 hours of <a href="https://www.apple.com/newsroom/2007/01/09Apple-Reinvents-the-Phone-with-iPhone/" target="_blank">Apple CEO Steve Jobs’s iPhone unveiling</a> in 2007, Nokia was already weighing its options. They’d immediately recognized the threat. However, outrunning it was another matter. </p><p>What follows is Nokia’s story over 14 years, from 1998 to 2012, as the world’s top cellphone maker—how its devices defined their time, how the tech reshaped what phones could be and do, and how the company’s good fortunes in the handset business came to an end. </p><h2>Nokia Was Once Unbeatable </h2><p>The centerpiece Nokia devices, the ones that people probably think of when they see the words “Nokia phone,” were the 3210 and its cousin, the 3310. <a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20160815174210/http:/www.techradar.com/us/news/phone-and-communications/mobile-phones/nokia-s-3310-the-greatest-phone-of-all-time-1287636" target="_blank">TechRadar has called the 3310 “the greatest phone of all time</a>.”</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="A left hand holds a Nokia 3210 phone with a Nokia store in the background." class="rm-shortcode" id="7a668" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-left-hand-holds-a-nokia-3210-phone-with-a-nokia-store-in-the-background.jpg?id=67118777&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">Nokia’s 3210 phone, released in 1999, was an inexpensive device aimed at younger users. </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Colin McPherson/Alamy </small></p><p>Released in 1999 and 2000, respectively, the two devices sold <a href="https://www.mobilephonemuseum.com/phone-detail/nokia-3210" target="_blank">more than 280 million units worldwide</a>. Their most innovative hardware feature was the <a href="https://www.mobilephonemuseum.com/phone-detail/nokia-3210" target="_blank">internal antenna</a>—the first mass-market phone without even a stub or retractable aerial. “Consumers had the perception that it could not work well without an external antenna,” said Peter Røpke, a former Nokia senior vice president, in a 2016 <a href="https://slate.com/technology/2016/09/the-development-of-the-nokia-3210-the-cellphone-that-started-the-mobile-revolution.html" target="_blank">interview with Slate</a>. </p><p>The phones shipped with games, including the legendary <a href="https://www.itsnicethat.com/features/taneli-armanto-the-history-of-snake-design-legacies-230221/" target="_blank"><em><em>Snake</em></em></a>, one of the most popular pre-smartphone mobile games—in which a pixelated serpent eats and grows with every morsel consumed.</p><p>Nokia introduced no small portion of the world to texting. At the time of the 3210 and 3310, the prevailing texting standard was <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SMS" target="_blank">SMS</a> (short message service), which allowed up to 160 characters per message. Nokia appended its own <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smart_Messaging" target="_blank">Nokia smart-messaging</a> service to SMS, which allowed the sending of small <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OTA_bitmap" target="_blank">bitmapped images across</a> an otherwise text-only system. A rich-text messaging system that allowed visual images, audio, and video followed in 2002, leading to a multimedia messaging service (MMS) standard that remains in place today. </p><p>Nokia also enabled users to easily create and share ringtones on their devices. By 2000, <a href="https://nokia.fandom.com/wiki/Composer" target="_blank">Nokia’s custom-ringtone Composer app</a> had popularized a new, short-form musical medium that the ringtone industry, at its peak, would transform into <a href="https://www.lightreading.com/business-management/ringtone-market-declines" target="_blank">a billion-dollar marketplace in the United States</a>. </p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image rm-float-left rm-resized-container rm-resized-container-25" style="float: left;"> <img alt="A face-on view of the Nokia 1100 feature phone, including a keypad and a black-and-white LCD screen.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="0e84b" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-face-on-view-of-the-nokia-1100-feature-phone-including-a-keypad-and-a-black-and-white-lcd-screen.jpg?id=67118794&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">Nokia introduced its 1100 phone in 2003 and ultimately sold half a billion units, making it the most popular cellphone in history.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Paul Chesne/Donaldson Collection/Getty Images </small></p><p>A few years later, Nokia reimagined its mobile handsets, releasing <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia_1100" target="_blank">the 1100</a> in 2003. The 1100 sold a half a billion units, more than <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_mobile_phones" target="_blank">any cellphone in history.</a> It <a href="https://capitaloneshopping.com/research/best-selling-products-of-all-time/" target="_blank">remains one of the best-selling consumer products ever</a>. Much of the 1100’s success was due to its price tag—<a href="https://www.phonearena.com/news/Did-you-know-that-the-Nokia-1100-is-the-worlds-best-selling-handset_id74364" target="_blank">in the neighborhood of US $100</a>, making it at the time <a href="https://www.downelink.com/the-nokia-1100-an-in-depth-history-of-the-legendary-brick-phone-that-connected-the-world/" target="_blank">Nokia’s most affordable device</a>.</p><p>Also contributing to the 1100’s popularity were features designed for longevity and tough environments, including dust resistance, nonslip sides for better handling in rainy conditions, and a 400-hour standby battery life. The 1100 introduced a flashlight as well, which the user turned on and off by holding down the “C” key. </p><p>Where most device makers at the time were worried about camera megapixels and color screens, Nokia had <a href="https://www.thecasecentre.org/products/view?id=92101" target="_blank">leapfrogged</a> its competition with a back-to-basics phone that could survive the rain, endure unreliable power grids, and light the way home.</p><h2>Apple Launched the iPhone, Nokia Scrambled  </h2><p><a href="https://archive.org/details/mw2007_iphone1000_100.1" target="_blank">On 9 January 2007</a>, at the Macworld conference in San Francisco, Steve Jobs made a <a href="https://archive.org/details/mw2007_iphone1000_100.1" target="_blank">characteristically bold claim</a>. “Today, Apple is reinventing the phone,” he said, soon pulling one of the first iPhones out of his pocket. </p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="Apple CEO Steve Jobs holds up one of the first iPhones while standing in front of an Apple logo.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="12f10" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/apple-ceo-steve-jobs-holds-up-one-of-the-first-iphones-while-standing-in-front-of-an-apple-logo.jpg?id=67118806&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">Apple CEO Steve Jobs famously launched the iPhone at the Macworld Conference in San Francisco on 9 January 2007. Nokia held a rapid-response meeting to the event the following day.   </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Tony Avelar/AFP/Getty Images </small></p><p>Rumors of Apple entering the phone market had swirled since the iPod’s debut in 2001, but nobody had really reckoned with what that might mean. </p><p>“Executive summary: Apple iPhone is a serious high-end contender,” read a <a href="https://repo.aalto.fi/uncategorized/IO_926740c7-5165-439a-a020-5205aeb4d4d5/" target="_blank">slide from a Nokia internal meeting</a> held the day after Jobs’s keynote. (That slide is now in <a href="https://repo.aalto.fi/index.php?name=SO_b66a9391-dcf8-4399-8e87-611f84c3fc4c" target="_blank">the company’s online archives</a>, opened to the public <a href="https://www.aalto.fi/en/nokia-design-archive/nokia-design-archive-making-twenty-years-of-design-history-public-to-the-world" target="_blank">last year</a>.)</p><p>“User interface has been a big strength for Nokia,” it continued. “Nokia needs to develop touch [user interface] to fight back.” </p><p><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/bryer/" target="_blank">Peter Bryer</a>, at the time Nokia’s manager of strategic foresight, was part of that 10 January meeting, and he recalls that Jobs’s announcement wasn’t unexpected. But the iPhone’s extensive reliance on multitouch—save for a single home button on the front—did surprise the team. </p><p>Nokia was already aware of multitouch technology, Bryer notes. In 2006, the U.S. computer scientist <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Han" target="_blank">Jeff Han</a> had given <a href="https://www.ted.com/talks/jeff_han_the_radical_promise_of_the_multi_touch_interface" target="_blank">a celebrated TED talk</a> about it, demonstrating a multitouch screen, which could sense multiple fingers on the screen at a time, not just one. Bryer remembers his colleague Timo Partanen, then Nokia’s director of market and competitor analysis, getting excited about Han’s demo.</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="A man stands in front of a tilted large electronic screen, whose reverse side is also visible.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="e5aae" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-man-stands-in-front-of-a-tilted-large-electronic-screen-whose-reverse-side-is-also-visible.jpg?id=67118813&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">In 2006, the NYU research scientist Jeff Han showed off a new multitouch interface technology as part of a popular TED talk. By the end of the decade, multitouch—in which multiple fingers can interact with a touchscreen at once—would play a key role in smartphones from Apple, HTC, and Palm.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit"><a href="https://www.flickr.com/photos/jurvetson/105123875/in/photostream/" target="_blank">Steve Jurvetson/Flickr</a> </small></p><p>“Timo burst into the room, saying, ‘You’ve got to see <a href="https://www.ted.com/talks/jeff_han_the_radical_promise_of_the_multi_touch_interface" target="_blank">this TED video of this guy using multitouch</a>,’” Bryer recalls. “We both thought that was cool and that’s the future. Then I looked at the sponsors of the presenter’s research, and among them were Nokia and Microsoft.”</p><p>And yet it took Nokia years to develop a phone that used multitouch. “Remember, Nokia is based in Finland,” he says. “It’s very cold in Finland. They wear gloves for six months of the year, including the executives. They didn’t think a device like that would work.”</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="Two hands wearing winter gloves hold a Nokia phone." class="rm-shortcode" id="f2932" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/two-hands-wearing-winter-gloves-hold-a-nokia-phone.jpg?id=67118814&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">Winter gloves were no obstacle to operating the chunky buttons on Nokia phones, a design priority perhaps stemming from the company’s Finnish culture and headquarters.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Erol Gurian/laif/Redux </small></p><p>Partanen was also at Nokia’s post-iPhone launch meeting, and recalls that there was little concern in the room. “We felt okay,” he says. “This is yet another competitor launching a great product. But we had no doubt that, if it’s successful, we would do the same. We will launch similar products.”</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image rm-float-left rm-resized-container rm-resized-container-25" style="float: left;"> <img alt="Two hands hold and interact with a touchscreen phone. The right hand uses a stylus to interface with the device.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="72e48" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/two-hands-hold-and-interact-with-a-touchscreen-phone-the-right-hand-uses-a-stylus-to-interface-with-the-device.jpg?id=67118823&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">In November 2008, Nokia released its first touchscreen phone, the 5800 Xpress Music, a year and a half after Apple had launched its iPhone.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Shaun Curry/AFP/Getty Images </small></p><p>That similar product ended up being the <a href="https://www.mobilephonemuseum.com/phone-detail/nokia-5800-xpressmusic" target="_blank">Nokia 5800 XpressMusic</a>, known as the Tube, released in 2008. “The idea was to focus on streaming videos and television,” Partanen says. “So we made a phone with a similar form factor to the iPhone [that was] optimized for streaming content.” But the 5800 was “delayed, delayed, delayed, delayed,” he says. “It didn’t materialize in the way it was planned. It was released as a watered-down version.”</p><p>Critics skewered the 5800’s “outdated” feature set and “ancient” <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S60_(software_platform)" target="_blank">S60 operating system</a>, which ran on top of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbian" target="_blank">Symbian OS</a>, an open-source mobile platform Nokia <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbian_Software#cite_note-Nokia_2008-06-24_Purchase-3" target="_blank">had recently acquired</a>. The 5800 <a href="https://www.marketwatch.com/story/cheap-cheerful-nokia-5800-proves" target="_blank">sold reasonably well</a> for its time, reaching around 8 million units in its first year alone. But it did not feature multitouch. </p><p>“I think that started to be the point when everybody realized that, hey, this is by far more difficult than earlier competitive issues we’ve had,” Partanen says.</p><p>Nokia finally released its <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia_N8" target="_blank">first device with multitouch</a> in 2010, three years after Jobs’s splashy iPhone announcement and four years after Han’s TED talk demo.</p><h2>How Android Ate Up the Low-End Market  </h2><p>Nokia had long owned the low end of the cellphone market, with its sturdy, no-frills devices suited for that segment. So the years immediately following the iPhone’s launch saw the Finnish firm continue to thrive as it kept turning out simple, rugged devices. </p><p>As <a href="https://www.cnet.com/reviews/nokia-1200-review/" target="_blank">one review</a> of the Nokia 1200—successor to the 1100—put it in October 2007, “This handset chucks away all the fancy features you’ve come to expect on a modern mobile, leaving you with a pared-down feature set that’s easy for tech novices to get their heads around.”</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="A man behind a wire screen holds up a Nokia phone to a user in the foreground, who looks at the device.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="43f43" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-man-behind-a-wire-screen-holds-up-a-nokia-phone-to-a-user-in-the-foreground-who-looks-at-the-device.jpg?id=67118830&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">Two cellphone users in Nairobi, Kenya in 2013 exchange a payment on a Nokia 1200 phone via the M-Pesa Mobile Money Market, a popular online banking service.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Trevor Snapp/Bloomberg/Getty Images </small></p><p>The 1200 kept the 1100’s dust-proofing, flashlight, and long-lasting battery, and added features aimed squarely at the developing world. The 1200 <a href="https://www.mobilephonemuseum.com/phone-detail/1200" target="_blank">was the first</a> to include call-time tracking and a multiuser phone book, allowing owners who planned to lend their device to set up call limits based on time or cost. This feature helped enable what Nokia researchers called kiosks—informal pay-per-call services, in which an enterprising phone subscriber charged neighbors and family members by the minute for use of the device.</p><p>In 2006, Nokia studied how Ugandans used their Nokia phones in rural and remote areas. <a href="https://repo.aalto.fi/uncategorized/IO_c703147d-bec7-4f0f-81e3-976b39bbde1b/" target="_blank">An internal company slide deck from the time</a> reveals just how keyed-in Nokia was to its lowest-income users. “Village phone operators are often women,” the slide deck notes. “And there tend to be a lot of children around. (Phones need to suffer considerable abuse from chewing, dust, sweat, etc.)”</p><p>“A unit of phone time is 60 seconds,” another slide states. “But to avoid accidentally going over that time and incurring extra costs, kiosk operators shorten the unit to 57 seconds, allowing a three-second margin of error. Shared mobile used as phone kiosk <em><em>must</em></em> show call time.”</p><p>Nokia’s familiarity with its market couldn’t protect the company forever, though.</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="Man in a village holds up a document with a detailed hand-drawn Nokia phone sketch." class="rm-shortcode" id="2e70f" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/man-in-a-village-holds-up-a-document-with-a-detailed-hand-drawn-nokia-phone-sketch.jpg?id=67118834&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">Nokia sought out user input around the world for the company’s device designs, including hosting “Open Studio” contests soliciting users’ sketches of their dream cellphone.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Shaul Schwarz/Getty Images </small></p><p>That’s because the iPhone wasn’t Nokia’s only looming smartphone competitor. In September 2008, the first Android phone went on sale—the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTC_Dream" target="_blank">HTC Dream, which was also sold as the T-Mobile G1</a>.</p><p>While the iPhone was aimed mostly at early adopters and affluent users who could afford to drop hundreds of dollars on a new phone, Android phones were, <a href="https://fortune.com/2010/12/22/2011-will-be-the-year-android-explodes/" target="_blank">within a couple </a><a href="https://fortune.com/2010/12/22/2011-will-be-the-year-android-explodes/" target="_blank">of </a><a href="https://fortune.com/2010/12/22/2011-will-be-the-year-android-explodes/" target="_blank">years</a>, aiming at the same low-cost, global user base Nokia was selling to.</p><p>“I think it’s fair to say Android is the one that disrupted the market more for Nokia,” Bryer says. “Most of Nokia’s successful devices were not on the high-end market. But then, when Android came along, it started to fill that lower end and eventually took that market away from us.”</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image rm-float-left rm-resized-container rm-resized-container-25" style="float: left;"> <img alt="A man holds two phones while standing in front of a large poster showing enlarged versions of the two devices.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="d158f" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-man-holds-two-phones-while-standing-in-front-of-a-large-poster-showing-enlarged-versions-of-the-two-devices.jpg?id=67118835&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">An executive from Nokia India in 2010 holds the company’s 5530 Xpress Music and 5230 phones, both of which had touchscreens, although only the 5530 had Wi-Fi.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Sam Panthaky/AFP/Getty Images </small></p><p>With two emerging competitors in the low end and high end, the Finnish device maker responded with a device that split the difference—and satisfied neither camp.</p><p>Released in 2009, the Nokia <a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20100611203042/http:/www.forum.nokia.com/Devices/Device_specifications/5230/" target="_blank">5230</a> attempted to be a low-priced, touchscreen (though not multitouch) competitor to both the iPhone and Android. It sold an impressive 150 million units, doing especially well in developing countries. </p><p>But the 5230 didn’t have Wi-Fi—<a href="https://www.cnet.com/reviews/nokia-5230-nuron-review/" target="_blank">one of the biggest complaints at the time</a>. In the developing world, Wi-Fi connections were still rare, so the lack of Wi-Fi made some sense. But <a href="https://www.techradar.com/reviews/phones/mobile-phones/nokia-5230-683869/review" target="_blank">the rest of the world</a> was <a href="http://www.allaboutsymbian.com/reviews/item/Nokia_5230.php" target="_blank">not pleased</a>. </p><p>“We had such a big gap and dominant position,” Bryer says. “Which does maybe create a level of comfort which you should never get.”</p><h2>How Nokia Lost the Smartphone Race  </h2><p>By the beginning of the 2010s, Nokia could have still drawn from the company’s labs, which were regularly spinning out new technologies and innovations. However, the Finnish handset maker ultimately failed to turn its R&amp;D into viable new product lines in response to the emerging smartphone threat.</p><p>Nokia’s predicament had precedent—Kodak, dominant in film photography, had actually <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Sasson#First_self-contained_digital_camera" target="_blank">invented the digital camera in 1975</a> but <a href="https://www.forbes.com/sites/chunkamui/2012/01/18/how-kodak-failed/" target="_blank">failed to commercialize it</a> before digital imaging made its core business obsolete.</p><p>“The technology coming from our R&amp;D teams was cutting edge,” says Gordon Murray-Smith, <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/gordonmurraysmith/details/experience/" target="_blank">director of services and ecosystems intelligence from 2008 to 2011</a>. He recalls attending annual R&amp;D innovation days that showcased work on self-healing materials and flexible screens, long before those technologies were seen elsewhere. “But why was Nokia not able to commercialize some of that really interesting and innovative activity more than it did?”</p><p>Nokia desperately needed an injection of life to change its fortunes. The company’s first non-Finnish CEO, <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Elop" target="_blank">Stephen Elop</a> (a Canadian fresh off a two-year stint on Microsoft’s leadership team), did not mince words. </p><p>In an internal <a href="https://aaplinvestors.net/iphone/nokiamemo/" target="_blank">memo from February 2011</a> that was soon <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Elop#%22Burning_Platform%22_memo" target="_blank">leaked to the media</a>, Elop wrote, “The first iPhone shipped in 2007, and we still don’t have a product that is close to their experience. Android came on the scene just over two years ago, and this week they took our leadership position in smartphone volumes. Unbelievable.”</p><p class="shortcode-media shortcode-media-rebelmouse-image"> <img alt="A hand holds a red Nokia touchscreen phone above a smart speaker device.  " class="rm-shortcode" id="0955c" src="https://spectrum.ieee.org/media-library/a-hand-holds-a-red-nokia-touchscreen-phone-above-a-smart-speaker-device.jpg?id=67118844&amp;width=980" /> <small class="image-media media-caption">In 2011, Nokia released the N9, a smartphone with a Linux-derived operating system. Within a year, Nokia had pivoted toward its Windows Phone-powered line of Lumia devices.  </small><small class="image-media media-photo-credit">Munshi Ahmed/Bloomberg/Getty Images </small></p><p>Elop oversaw the 2011 launch of a Linux-based smartphone, the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia_N9" target="_blank">Nokia N9</a>. The N9 ran on a distribution of Linux called <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MeeGo" target="_blank">MeeGo</a>. Reviewers at the time praised the new smartphone direction the Finnish phone maker had taken. “Possibly the most beautiful phone ever made,” <a href="https://www.engadget.com/2011-10-22-nokia-n9-review.html" target="_blank">wrote one reviewer</a> about the N9 for Engadget.</p><p>But the N9’s accolades did not ultimately carry the day. Nokia announced its <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nokia_Lumia" target="_blank">Lumia line</a> of phones the same year—a direct pivot away from MeeGo toward the Windows Phone. It would be the last major strategic turn Nokia would take as a cellphone manufacturer. From this point forward, a succession of C-suite decisions all but sealed the fate of Nokia’s iconic line of phones. </p><p>In 2013, Microsoft announced its <a href="https://news.microsoft.com/source/2013/09/03/microsoft-to-acquire-nokias-devices-services-business-license-nokias-patents-and-mapping-services/" target="_blank">bid to acquire Nokia’s handset operations</a>. After the sale went through the following year, it rebranded the division Microsoft Mobile. But the year after that, Microsoft decided it had made a costly mistake, <a href="https://news.microsoft.com/source/2015/07/08/microsoft-announces-restructuring-of-phone-hardware-business/" target="_blank">writing down $7.6 billion</a>—<a href="https://www.computerworld.com/article/1620732/microsoft-writes-off-76b-admits-failure-of-nokia-acquisition-2.html" target="_blank">nearly what it paid for Nokia’s handset division</a>—and <a href="https://www.reuters.com/article/business/microsoft-hangs-up-on-nokia-business-to-cut-7800-jobs-idUSKCN0PI0GI/" target="_blank">laying off</a> nearly half <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2014/07/18/business/microsoft-to-cut-up-to-18000-jobs.html" target="_blank">of the former Nokia staff</a> it had inherited.</p><p>In 2016, Microsoft <a href="https://www.hmd.com/en_int/press/hmd-global-founded" target="_blank">sold its feature phone assets to HMD Global</a>. The latter still sells <a href="https://www.hmd.com/en_int/nokia-3210" target="_blank">Nokia-branded phones</a>—<a href="https://www.hmd.com/en_int/nokia-105-2024" target="_blank">budget-friendly devices</a> as well as <a href="https://www.hmd.com/en_int/feature-phones-series/retro-phone" target="_blank">nostalgia reproductions</a> of models from Nokia’s glory days. What remained was a brand name, some intellectual property, and two decades of hard-won lessons about what it takes to stay on top—and what it costs when you can’t.</p><p>“When you look at the players in the world of smartphones today, any of those players would struggle ever to achieve 14 consecutive years of being No. 1,” says Murray-Smith. </p><p>Partanen says there was a downside to Nokia’s mobile-phone dominance. “Often, being the first mover is not necessarily the best position,” he says. “Being a quick follower is the best position.”</p><p>The company itself ultimately survived, even if the transition wasn’t painless. Nokia’s revenues, which <a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/technology-23947212" target="_blank">peaked in 2007</a>, fell sharply through the mid-2010s before <a href="https://www.nokia.com/system/files/files/nokia_ar16_full_report_english_3.pdf" target="_blank">its new business line</a>—telecom infrastructure—took off. Nokia now ranks among the world’s top three suppliers of 5G network equipment, serving carriers <a href="https://www.rcrwireless.com/20251107/5g/telia-5g-nokia" target="_blank">across more than 125 countries</a>, alongside <a href="https://www.ericsson.com/en/networks" target="_blank">Ericsson</a> and <a href="https://www.huawei.com/en/public-policy/ict-infrastructure" target="_blank">Huawei</a>. Although the company could never quite crack the smartphone, it now plays a key role in providing the network backbone those smartphones run on. <span class="ieee-end-mark"></span></p>

title: "Pan Jianwei becomes first Chinese scientist to win the top UN basic science prize"
---
Chinese quantum scientist Pan Jianwei has won the United Nations’ top fundamental science prize for his work in quantum computation and communications, Unesco has announced.
Pan is a laureate of the third Unesco-Russia Mendeleev International Prize in the Basic Sciences, an award commemorating Russian chemist Dmitri Mendeleev, creator of the periodic table.
The annual award, which is funded by the Russian government, honours two scientists for discoveries and innovations in basic sciences that...

sources:
  - source: "The Decoder"
    url: "https://the-decoder.com/nadella-calls-out-ai-labs-like-openai-and-anthropic-for-banning-distillation-while-training-on-everyone-elses-data"
title: "Nadella calls out AI labs like OpenAI and Anthropic for banning distillation while training on everyone else's data"
---
<p><img alt="" class="attachment-full size-full wp-post-image" height="768" src="https://the-decoder.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/microsoft_datacenter.png" style="height: auto; margin-bottom: 10px;" width="1376" /></p>
<p>        Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella is calling out OpenAI and Anthropic for what he calls a "reverse information paradox." They train on public data under fair use but ban distillation of their own models, all while learning from customer interactions. Nadella wants companies to control their own learning infrastructure. Microsoft, of course, sells exactly that.</p>
<p>The article <a href="https://the-decoder.com/nadella-calls-out-ai-labs-like-openai-and-anthropic-for-banning-distillation-while-training-on-everyone-elses-data/">Nadella calls out AI labs like OpenAI and Anthropic for banning distillation while training on everyone else&#039;s data</a> appeared first on <a href="https://the-decoder.com">The Decoder</a>.</p>

sources:
  - source: "Bloomberg Markets"
    url: "https://bloomberg.com/news/videos/2026-07-13/mgm-up-on-takeover-bid-sk-hynix-down-on-memory-pullback-video"
title: "MGM Rises on Takeover Bid; SK Hynix Falls on Memory Pullback | Stock Movers"
---
On this episode of Stock Movers:

- MGM Resorts International (MGM) shares are rising on a report regarding a potential takeover bid from Barry Diller.
- SK Hynix (SKHY) shares are lower and other memory stocks are also falling. It is lower over concerns with the sustainability of artificial-intelligence spending. The drop in South Korean trading is likely to cause a hit to the American depositary receipts as well. The debate between the optimists and pessimists will continue, which should create volatility, according to Hendi Susanto, portfolio manager at Gabelli Funds.
- Agenus (AGEN) shares are moving higher on news of a deal that it's selling up to $3540 million worth of securities. (Source: Bloomberg)

Samsung takes particular pride in its OLED tech, which spans the globe's most advanced display-related offerings. Even so, given the volumes that Samsung produces at scale, some shortcomings are bound to emerge, as is the case with a troubling red tint issue that some Galaxy S26 Ultra units are now apparently contending with. What's more, this issue has gained added significance in the wake of Samsung's ambitions related to a rollable phone. UBS Group AG analyst Erika Najarian says Goldman Sachs Group Inc. may have the tallest order when it comes to demonstrating earnings prowess this week, while investors may be focused on more on succession at JPMorgan Chase & Co. She previews Wall Street bank earnings on "Bloomberg Surveillance." (Source: Bloomberg)

title: "Paramount-Warner Bros. Deal Challenged by California, States"
---
A group of 12 states sued Paramount Skydance Corp. Monday seeking to block its $110 billion bid to buy Warner Bros. Discovery Inc., alleging the blockbuster Hollywood deal would leave viewers with higher prices and fewer choices for movies and television.  Jennifer Rie, Bloomberg Intelligence Senior Litigation Analyst for Antitrust joins Bloomberg Businessweek Daily to discuss.
